## [2026-08-13] User

> yes fix that

### Claude

Same root cause for all four. Checking whether other dialogs store tuples the same way.

Only one site. Let me look at the context to fix it cleanly.

Now the three tests that read `data(0, Qt.UserRole)` directly:

Adding a regression test that pins the contract on both PySide6 behaviours:

**2563 passed, 0 failed** — baseline restored. Committing.

Fixed and pushed as `f508169a`. Suite is back to **2563 passed, 0 failed**.

**What it was:** the unused-assets tree stores each leaf's `(asset_type, asset_name)` pair with `setData(0, Qt.UserRole, (category, name))` at [asset_dialogs.py:543](widgets/asset_tree/asset_dialogs.py#L543). Qt round-trips that through QVariant, and PySide6 6.10 hands back a **list** where 6.9 handed back the tuple it was given. Three failures were tuple-vs-list comparisons; the fourth was a genuine `TypeError: unhashable type: 'list'` from putting the pair in a set.

So the tests were written on a box with 6.9 and only break on 6.10 — which is why this sat unnoticed. Nothing in the dialog itself cared: `_delete_selected` only unpacks the pair, `_update_button_states` only takes its bool. But the contract was silently version-dependent, and the next caller to hash one would hit the same wall.

**The fix** is to normalise on read rather than trust `setData` to hand back what it was given: a new `UnusedAssetsDialog.item_key(item)` returns a real tuple (or `None` for category/placeholder rows), and `_checked_items` goes through it. I swept the repo for the same hazard — this was the only `UserRole` store holding a tuple; every other one holds a string, dict or path.

Tests now read the pair through `item_key`, plus 4 new ones pinning the normalisation against **both** stored shapes explicitly. A `setData` round-trip test alone only exercises whichever conversion the installed PySide6 does, so it wouldn't catch a regression on the other version — worth doing given this bug's whole nature was being invisible on one of them.

Worth noting for the CLAUDE.md baseline: that file documents Windows green snapshots but this box is now on PySide6 6.10.1, and this is exactly the class of breakage a version bump produces. Both untracked session-note transcripts are still uncommitted if you want them synced.

Block World is where it was — Phase 2a done, 2b (multi-layer heightmap) next, with the transparency findings from the preview as input.

**Phase 2b is done and pushed** (`12949c31`). Blocks stack: walls can be several blocks high, you can stand on things and see over what's below you, and blocks show their top faces. Suite **2592 passed, 0 failed** (+29).

The whole vertical projection turned out to be one line, now documented in [renderer.py](extensions/block_world/renderer.py)'s docstring:

```
y = horizon + (eye_z - zval) * (screen_h * cell_size / distance)
```

Phase 2a is exactly the `eye_z = 0.5`, one-layer case of it — which is what made a compatibility proof possible.

**What changed**

- `march_ray` is now the single DDA, yielding each cell's **entry and exit** distance (a top face runs from the near vertical face back to the far one, so it needs both). `cast_ray` became a thin first-hit wrapper for Phase 3 picking.
- Cells collect near→far so a gapless-stack early-out can stop the march, then paint far→near. The early-out deliberately won't fire on a stack with a hole — that column can be seen through.
- Horizontal faces are flat-shaded from the texture's average colour. Per-pixel floor casting is the expensive step the raycast arc deferred, and a step or pit reads fine without it.
- `state.column_index` is a derived, cache-invalidated heightmap. Probing the `"x,y,z"` string keys layer-by-layer would cost tens of thousands of string formats per frame.

**A pre-existing bug fixed en route:** textured strips scale to a *rounded* height while the span they fill is *ceiled*, so they could fall a row short — 2a showed the flat floor colour along the bottom edge of walls.

**On the compatibility claim**, which I got wrong twice before getting it right. A single-layer opaque world is identical to 2a across 160 frames; all 51,649 differing pixels out of 76.8M are seam rows 2a left unpainted, classified per pixel with a run-length check rather than eyeballed. Two deliberate exceptions, each tested: the seam fill, and **alpha textures now compositing with the block behind them** instead of the flat sky — that's the exact complaint from the 2a preview review, fixed as a side effect of painting far→near.

Two process notes worth keeping:

- My first "identical" run showed 60 differing frames from a **1-ULP** difference: 2a recovered the entry distance as `(side + delta) - delta` after stepping, not by capturing `side` before it. Enough to move a strip edge a pixel. `march_ray` now reproduces 2a's arithmetic deliberately, with a comment so nobody tidies it away.
- I mutation-checked the tests rather than trusting green, and **the seam test survived** — it scanned for background rows *between* a wall's first and last drawn row, and the shortfall is at the bottom edge, so it was structurally blind to the bug it was written for. Rewritten to assert the exact last painted row. Same lesson as the minimap marker: assert *where* an edge lands, not that something drew.

The preview tool now has the multi-layer half of the scene (staircase, terrace, pit, tower) plus step-up movement — `--shots` writes 14 frames. I checked them; the staircase and the "see over the walls from up top" overlook both read correctly. **The pit viewpoint I kept deliberately as evidence of the 2c limitation:** a level camera can't look down, so a pit at your feet falls below the frame — worth having a picture of rather than a paragraph about, when 2c comes up for decision.

Next per the plan is **Phase 3** (place/break blocks + hotbar), with 2c still deferred. Worth a look at the new frames before I start.

Fixed and pushed (`0d8bee06`). Both things you hit were the same root cause.

**Walking on top of walls wasn't a movement bug.** Every wall in the scene was exactly one block tall, and in a world of unit cubes a one-block wall and a one-block step are *the same object* — any rule permissive enough to climb a staircase must also climb a kerb. So height is the only thing that can separate them, which is exactly your second point.

Heights now, with a step at one block:

| | Blocks | Climbable |
|---|---|---|
| Perimeter wall | 4 | no — a step is ¼ of it |
| Corridor / hut | 3 | no |
| Pillars | 2, 3, 4 | no |
| Display rows | 2 | no |
| Staircase riser | 1 | **yes** — the only one in the world |

`MAX_STEP_UP` stays at 1 and now does the right thing everywhere. I verified per feature rather than by eye: every wall reports `enterable=False` from ground level, and walking east along the stairs goes 0→1→2→3→4→5 onto the deck.

The terrace rose with it — the deck is at layer 5 now, five steps up — so it still overlooks the 3-high corridor walls, which is the whole point of that viewpoint. The corridor also reads far better as a proper 3-high passage than it did as a one-block kerb.

One thing I couldn't fix by moving the camera, so I labelled it honestly instead: **a staircase seen head-on correctly renders as a stepped wall**, because each tread hides behind the riser in front of it. Seeing the profile needs a side-on view from about 10 cells back — at cell size 32 on a 600px screen, five stacked blocks only fit in frame at that range — and this world has no clear sightline that long. I tried eight camera placements before concluding the scene doesn't afford the shot. The `steps` viewpoint now says so and points you at the walkaround, where the HUD layer counter ticks up as you climb. Both lessons are in the plan doc, since Phase 5 will need them when it designs a sample around something the player is meant to look at.

## [2026-08-13] User

> The transparent block works OK from the side but not straight on when the player moves close to the transparent block

### Claude

That's a real bug, and the screenshots pin it precisely. Let me confirm the mechanism before fixing.

Confirmed. At 0.5 cells the glass fills the screen, so the occlusion early-out fires and the march stops — the transparent texels then composite against bare sky/floor instead of the blocks behind. At 1.5+ cells it doesn't fire, which is why it looks right from further back and side-on. `BLOCK_TYPES` already carries the flag the early-out should have been consulting.

Now a regression test — including a control proving the optimisation still fires for opaque blocks:

Verifying the new test fails without the fix, and rendering your exact camera position to confirm visually:

Fixed — the glass now shows the water block behind it at point-blank, matching how it looks side-on. Updating the docs that claimed the flag was unread:

Good catch — that was a real bug, and your "from the side but not straight on close" framing is what pinpointed it. Fixed and pushed (`93d3ee07`).

**The compositing was never the problem; the occlusion early-out was.** A column can be seen through two ways — a hole in the stack, or a transparent block — and `_fully_covers` only excluded the first. Walking up to the glass made it big enough to satisfy "this stack covers the whole column", which **stopped the ray march**, so the blocks it should have composited against were never drawn at all. It fell back to the bare sky/floor fill.

Measured on your exact geometry, the glass being 2 blocks tall:

| Distance | px per cell | March stops? |
|---|---|---|
| 0.5 cells | 1200 | **yes** ← your screenshot 3 |
| 1.5 cells | 400 | no |
| 2.5+ cells | ≤240 | no |

That distance threshold is precisely why it looked fine until you got close, and why it read as a texture glitch rather than an occlusion one.

The fix uses a flag that was already there: `BLOCK_TYPES` has carried `transparent` since Phase 1 with nothing reading it. `state.is_transparent` now exposes it and the early-out consults it. I re-rendered your three camera positions — at point-blank the glass now shows the **water block** behind it, matching the side-on view.

Four tests, including a control that an opaque stack *still* stops the march — otherwise the "fix" could just be quietly disabling the optimisation. Mutation-checked: removing the guard fails two of them. Suite **2596 passed, 0 failed**.

I also generalised the lesson in the plan doc, because 2c and the Phase 6 export ports will each re-derive this logic: **any occlusion shortcut has to ask whether a block can be seen through, not just whether it's there.** I'd written the hole case in from the start and simply didn't think of the transparent one — and no test caught it because every test I wrote used opaque blocks.

Worth another run to confirm it looks right to you at point-blank, and the wall-height change from the last round while you're in there.

### Claude

Confirmed. Textured tops are what I'd deferred as 2c work — but `raycast_2_5d` already does per-pixel floor casting on desktop, so there's an established pattern here rather than inventing one. Let me look at it.

Same low-res-cast-then-upscale approach, adapted per-column. Writing the textured horizontal face:

Now wiring it into the render loop:

Now let me look at it, and measure the cost — this is the expensive path I'd deferred:

The deck reads as real paving now. Let me add the single-sample fast path (res=4 and res=8 costing the same says per-face overhead dominates, not the sampling loop), and expose the knob on the action:

The fast path was within noise, so the cost is elsewhere. Let me find out where before deciding whether to chase it.

The profile is unambiguous: `block_face_textures` is called **60,905 times per frame** and does an `os.path.join` every time. That's a pure function over a static registry — memoising it is the fix.

20% faster, and it speeds the pre-existing flat path too. Now tests for the textured tops:

The eye height put the face below the 240px test surface. Fixing the geometry:

The grass test survived a mutant that swaps in the side texture — with the camera looking straight down +x the sampled row is constant, so colour can't distinguish them. Testing the wiring directly instead:

One gap: nothing covers the underside face's texture. Adding it:

All four mutants caught now. Full suite and a final look:

Textured deck and wall tops. Updating the docs that promised flat shading:

Done and pushed (`883527c5`). Top and bottom faces are texture-mapped now — the terrace deck reads as real paving instead of a grey slab.

**How it works.** Inverting the projection gives the distance to a horizontal plane for a screen row directly:

```
y = horizon + (eye_z - zval) * (h * cell / dist)   →   dist = (eye_z - plane_z) * h * cell / (y - horizon)
```

So each row of a face is a known distance along that column's ray, and the world point there gives the texel. Sampled every *N* rows into a one-pixel column and upscaled — the same trick `raycast_2_5d.cast_floor_plane` already uses, and `top_cast_res` is the same knob as its `floor_cast_res` (default 4; **0 restores the flat fill** if you want the speed back).

**On performance — I profiled rather than guessed, and was wrong about where the time went.** Not the ray march, not the per-column blits: `cProfile` on one frame of the terrace view found **60,905 calls to `block_face_textures`**, each doing an `os.path.join`, on a pure function over a static registry. Memoising it took ~20% off every frame, textured *and* flat. Net effect:

| View | Before | Now (textured) |
|---|---|---|
| terrace | 52 ms | 55 ms |
| overlook | 27 ms | 28 ms |
| corridor | 40 ms | 35 ms |

So texturing is roughly free relative to where you were.

**What I measured but deliberately did not fix: overdraw.** That frame marches 18.6 cells and draws 38.1 wall strips *per column* for a handful of visible surfaces, because painting far→near rasterises everything hidden. Deck-heavy views sit at ~18 fps. The sound fix is a cumulative-coverage early-out, and there's a subtlety worth recording before anyone tries it: the union of per-cell spans is only guaranteed contiguous for stacks **taller than the eye** (those all contain the horizon row), so a naive min/max accumulator is wrong for a world of low blocks. That's in the plan doc and the README's "what's not here yet".

Mutation-checking the new tests found two gaps I'd otherwise have shipped:

- The obvious grass test — "the top face should be green, unlike its dirt-banded side" — **passed while being fed the side texture**. A camera looking straight down +x holds the sampled row constant at v=0.5, where `grass_side` happens to also be green. Replaced with a spy on which Surface actually reaches the rasteriser.
- Nothing covered the underside face's texture at all.

Suite **2603 passed, 0 failed**. Worth a walk around the terrace to see the paving.

> Approach this as the design lead at a small studio known for their versatility, giving every client a visual identity pitched at the treatment the task actually calls for. Make deliberate choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this subject, and avoid templated designs.
> 
> ## Read the request first
> 
> Calibrate treatment, not whether to design. A doc deserves the same craft as a landing page — what changes is the treatment that craft is delivered in.
> 
> Many requests call for a more utilitarian treatment: a plan, a memo, a demo. Make it polished: include real typographic hierarchy, considered spacing, and a proper palette, but avoid over-designing. Most pages do not need a flashy, gigantic hero. Keep flourishes tasteful and limited.
> 
> Some requests call for an editorial treatment: a landing page, a game, an app or tool they'll keep or share.
> 
> When unsure: a well-composed page is never the wrong answer; an over-designed visual identity sometimes is.
> 
> Fundamentals below apply to everything. The editorial process after that runs only when the read above says so.
> 
> ## Fundamentals for every artifact
> 
> **Honor what's already there** Look for an existing design system first — CLAUDE.md, a tokens or theme file, existing component styles. When one exists, apply it; everything below fills gaps and never overrides. Precedence is always: the user's own words, then the project's existing system, then your choices.
> 
> **Ground it in the subject.** If the subject isn't already clear, pin it: one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job. The subject's own world — its materials, instruments, vernacular — is where distinctive choices come from. Build with real content throughout, never lorem.
> 
> **Pair typefaces** Typography carries the page even when the page isn't about typography. The Artifact CSP blocks font CDNs, so don't link a webfont URL and risk a silent fallback. Instead inline the face as a @font-face data URI. Keep running text near 65 characters wide; set a type scale and stay on it; give headings `text-wrap: balance`, body text room to breathe, and uppercase labels a touch of letter-spacing.
> 
> **Choose neutrals, don't default to them.** A pure mid-grey reads as unconsidered; a grey with a slight hue bias toward the page's accent reads as chosen. Pure white and near-black are fine grounds when they suit the subject — the point is that the neutral was picked, not inherited.
> 
> **Design both themes.** The page renders in the viewer's theme, and the viewer has three states, not two: an explicit choice stamps `data-theme="dark"` / `data-theme="light"` on the root element, and the default "system" setting stamps *nothing* — most viewers see the un-stamped document, where only `prefers-color-scheme` separates light from dark. Structure the CSS token-level for all three: the bare `:root` block defines the complete light palette (for a deliberately dark-first design, swap light and dark consistently through this whole pattern); `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` redefines only the tokens, guarded as `:root:not([data-theme="light"])` so an explicit light choice beats a dark OS; `:root[data-theme="dark"]` redefines them again so the toggle also wins in the other direction. Style components through the tokens, never directly inside a media or `[data-theme]` block — a color whose only definition sits behind `[data-theme]` never applies in the un-stamped state, and the page renders one theme's text on the other theme's ground. Two more rules keep each theme resolving as a set: the artifact composites over a ground the viewer paints in *its* theme, so `body` must set an explicit `background` from a token — a transparent body silently borrows the host's ground; and every element that sets a color takes it from the same token set as the surface behind it, never a literal that only works in one theme. Before publishing, scan the stylesheet for any color declared only inside a media or `[data-theme]` block — that is the classic unreadable-artifact bug. Give the second theme the same care as the first — don't naively invert; keep contrast legible and the accent working on both grounds. A design that deliberately commits to one visual world (a neon arcade screen, a letterpress invitation) may stay single-theme — then skip the media query and stamps entirely but still paint the background and every color explicitly, so the page holds on either host ground; make it a choice, not an omission.
> 
> **Let layout do the spacing.** Lay out sibling groups with flex or grid and `gap`, not per-element margins that silently collapse or double. Wide content — tables, code, diagrams — gets `overflow-x: auto` on its own container so the page body never scrolls sideways. Reach for `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` wherever digits line up in columns.
> 
> **Avoid AI-generated design** AI-generated design currently clusters around a few looks: warm cream (#F4F1EA) with a serif display and terracotta accent; near-black with a lone acid-green or vermilion pop; broadsheet hairline rules with dense columns; a purple-to-blue gradient hero on white; Inter or Space Grotesk as the "safe" face; emoji as section markers; everything centered; `rounded-lg` everywhere; accent bar/rail on rounded cards. Where the user pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly — their words always win, including when they ask for one of these looks. Where nothing is specified, don't spend that freedom on one of these defaults.
> 
> **Build cleanly** Be cognizant of overlapping elements, cascade collisions, silent font fallbacks; visual bugs hide in the gap between source and output. Close every non-void element, double-quote attributes, give keyboard focus a visible state, respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. For generative or decorative graphics, reach for Canvas or WebGL rather than hand-authoring long SVG path data.
> 
> **CSS rules** When writing the CSS, watch your selector specificities. It is easy to generate classes that cancel each other out — a type-based selector like `.section` fighting an element-based one like `.cta` over padding and margins between sections. Structure the cascade so it doesn't silently undo your spacing.
> 
> **Writing the copy** Words are design material, not decoration. Write from the user's side of the screen — name things by what people recognize, not how the system is built (a person manages *notifications*, not *webhook config*). Active voice; a control says exactly what happens ("Publish", then a toast that says "Published"). Errors explain what went wrong and how to fix it — no apologies, no vagueness. Specific beats clever.
> 
> **Name the page like a product, not a caption.** The `<title>` is the artifact's name in the gallery and the browser tab, and it sets the reader's first impression of care. Give the page a real name: a short noun phrase, typically two to four words, specific to the subject — or, for a page that exists to answer one question, that question itself, which is then the page's name. Stop at the name — a title that carries its own explainer after a dash or colon reads as generated filler. The name must also identify the page among many: in the gallery it sits beside dozens of other artifacts, and a generic category label that could sit on any of them fails as a name just as surely as an appended explainer. When a candidate title pairs the name with a generic word — a greeting, a category, a page-type label — the name is the half to keep; a trim that drops the identity and keeps the generic word produces exactly the title that could sit on any page. And the rule removes explainers, it does not impose brevity: a multi-word title that already reads as one specific name is finished, and shortening it further only makes it generic. The one-sentence publish `description` is where the explanation belongs; the gallery shows it right under the title.
> 
> **Structure is information** Structural devices, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, should encode something true about the content, not decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that's only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence - like a real process or a typed timeline where order carries information the reader needs. Question if choices like numbered markers actually make sense before incorporating them.
> 
> **When it's a UI, not a document** A dashboard or tool is scanned and operated, not read top-to-bottom, so the craft shifts from typography to information design. Surface the summary before the detail; encode state in form as well as number — a pill, a chip, a severity stripe — so what needs attention reads at a glance. Semantic color (good / warning / critical) is separate from the accent hue and doesn't count as your accent. Give sparklines and charts the same care as type: an area fill, a faint grid, an emphasized endpoint. What's interactive should look interactive.
> 
> 
> 
> ## Process
> 
> Before writing code, sketch a short design plan — a compact token system with color, type, and layout:
> - **Color**: describe the palette as 4–6 named hex values.
> - **Type**: typefaces for 2+ roles — a characterful display face used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed.
> - **Layout**: a layout concept in one or two sentences.
> 
> Then build, following the plan and deriving every color and type decision from it.
> 
> ## When the request is editorial
> 
> The stance shifts: the client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view. Make opinionated calls, and take one real aesthetic risk where it serves the work.
> 
> Review the design plan against the subject before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page, revise that part, and note what you changed and why. Only after you've confirmed the plan's uniqueness do you write the code, following the revised plan exactly.
> 
> **Principles** 
> 
> - The hero is a thesis: open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world — headline, image, live demo, interactive moment. 
> - Typography carries the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, not the same families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content. 
> - Leverage motion deliberately. Think about where and if animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, a scroll-triggered reveal, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. An orchestrated moment usually lands harder than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation contributes to the feeling that the design is AI-generated. 
> - Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well.
> - Spend your boldness in one place; keep everything around it quiet. If the accent fights the ground, shift it toward analogous or drop saturation rather than replacing it.

**What landed**

`renderer.pick_block` marches the *same* `march_ray` at the *same* angle the renderer draws its centre column from, returning both the cell to break and the cell to build in. That reuse is why the crosshair and the picked block can't drift apart — and the tests check the rendered frame changes, not just the world dict.

Two actions in the "3D View" category. `place_block` offers the 30 registry block types as a **dropdown**, so a typo can't become a silent runtime no-op.

**One design point worth knowing, because it's counterintuitive.** `place_block` does *not* check whether the target cell is empty, and must not: `pick_block` only advances past cells it has read as air, and an occupied first cell returns immediately with no placement — so a returned placement is air *by construction*. I shipped that guard in my first draft, and mutation testing exposed it as **unreachable code posing as a safety net**. Removed, invariant documented where it's actually guaranteed, and pinned by a parametrised test over six world layouts.

**And a limitation that falls out of 2b, not a bug:** the ray is horizontal at eye height, so only your own layer is reachable. You build outwards at your feet and climb what you built. Digging down or placing onto ground below you needs 2c's free look. Standing flush against a wall genuinely has nowhere to build at your layer, and the action does nothing.

**Three of six mutants survived my first test pass** — one root cause. My test helper carried its own copy of the handler's camera resolution, and every action test faced angle 0 on layer 0, where a flipped angle sign and a hardcoded layer are both invisible. Fixed with action-level tests at a non-zero facing and non-zero layer; all six caught now. That's the same failure mode as the grass-texture test earlier today: a helper that duplicates the code under test, testing itself.

### Claude

That's the right instinct, and it's worth pinning down now because it changes *where* the feature belongs. Let me separate two things that look like one.

**Authoring and playing are different moments.** Whether a *player* can break blocks isn't an engine mode at all — it's the author's choice, made by whether they bind `break_block` to an input event. The preview feels dangerous because it's a harness with no event system, so it binds the mouse unconditionally. A real authored game only has breaking if someone wired it up.

Edit mode, meanwhile, is the **visual world editor** the plan already defers out of Phase 3 — and that's exactly where undo belongs.

So I'd argue against a global mode flag in the engine: it adds a concept without adding capability. What the engine genuinely lacks is a way to say *this block cannot be broken* — `BLOCK_TYPES` already carries `solid` and `transparent`, and `breakable` is the natural third. Let me record that, then fix the immediate pain in the preview.

Now the immediate pain — undo, and a lock so you can explore without wrecking anything:

`BLOCK_TYPES` now carries `breakable` (default True) alongside `solid` and `transparent`. `state.is_breakable` reads it, `break_block` checks it, and **nothing else does** — an unbreakable block is still targeted (the crosshair lights up, which is the feedback saying it's there and solid), still occludes, still gets built against, and can still be **placed**. An author lining a world's edge with the stuff has to be able to put it there in the first place. Swinging at one is a silent no-op, same as swinging at air.

**`obsidian` is the designated boundary material.** A flag no type ever sets would be exactly the unreachable-code-posing-as-a-safety-net that mutation testing caught in `place_block` this morning, so it needed a real user from the start. Obsidian is the only near-black stone in the registry, so *"the black one can't be broken"* reads at a glance with no HUD to explain it — and it reuses an already-audited texture, so no new `ASSETS.md` row. An unknown block id counts as breakable, so a typo can't silently produce indestructible scenery.

The walkaround honours the flag itself, since it calls the state API directly rather than dispatching actions — without that, the demo would contradict the engine. There's an obsidian block in the shiny row at ground level if you want to try swinging at it.

Turn on build mode with `B` and you'll see a faint trapezoid on the floor marking the cell the block will land in. It tracks as you turn, and disappears when there's nowhere valid to build.

**The important part is that it's not an approximation.** `draw_cell_outline` rides on a new `project_point`, which is the exact inverse of what the render loop does per column — same camera-plane depth, same one-line vertical mapping. So the outline sits on the geometry rather than near it. I verified that against the *rasteriser*, not just the formula: a test projects a wall's top edge and checks the wall is actually drawn there.

That's reusable. Anything later that needs to draw *into* the 3D view — a selection box, a marker, a highlighted face — should go through `project_point` instead of re-deriving screen positions.

It's drawn through a small translucent surface sized to the outline's own bounding box, so a faint line costs a few hundred pixels a frame rather than a screen-sized allocation. And it skips entirely when a corner falls behind the camera plane — a partly-behind quad projects to nonsense, and half an outline is worse than none.

**Mutation testing found two more real gaps**, both the same shape as before — the tests, not the code:

- Every projection test used eye height 0.5, so **hardcoding that constant passed everything**. It would have misplaced every overlay the moment you stood on the terrace. Fixed by parametrising over eye height as well as block height.
- The outline tests only checked that ink landed, so **a quad collapsing to a triangle went unnoticed**. Now all four corners are pinned individually.

And a third "survivor" that turned out to be my own harness lying to me: the string I was swapping also appeared in the function's docstring, so a first-occurrence replace mutated the prose and left the code untouched. The harness now asserts its anchor is unique — that's in the plan doc, since it would have sent a future session hunting for a nonexistent test gap.

One thing I deliberately left: the outline is drawn only by the walkaround. Exposing it to authored games as an action, so a building game doesn't reimplement it, is noted as a follow-up alongside the hotbar and the world generator.

Fixed and pushed (`ecb319cd`). Suite **2677 passed, 0 failed**.

That wasn't an edge case — it was a **dead end**, and worse than it looked. Placement was "the empty cell before the first hit", and a one-block-thick wall has no cell before the hit that *is* the hole: the ray goes straight through and targets whatever's beyond, so the block lands past it. I reproduced it from the corridor side, from the yard side, and from further back — unfillable in every case. In your screenshot the ray was passing through the hole and finding the hut wall two cells beyond, which is why the outline sat out in the open.

**The fix** uses the signal that distinguishes damage from open air: your hole still had two cobble blocks resting on top of it. `pick_block` now prefers the first **gap** the ray passes through — an empty cell at your layer with a block above it — over the cell before the target. An open doorway has nothing above it, so it's never bricked up by accident.

Breaking is deliberately unaffected: the crosshair still reaches *past* the gap, so what's genuinely behind the hole stays targetable and breakable. Only where a new block lands changes — and the outline shows you exactly where.

**One case it doesn't cover, which I'd rather flag than paper over:** a hole knocked out of the *top* of a wall has nothing above it to mark it as a gap. Reaching that properly needs 2c's free look. It's in the plan doc.

Worth noting on process: **my tests were wrong twice before the code was.** First I put the "beyond" block outside the default reach so the ray never got there; then I put it directly in front of the camera approaching from the other side. Both looked like code failures at a glance. The layout is now spelled out as a comment in the test class, because the geometry is the hard-to-read part. All five mutants caught.

### Claude

Two problems, and the second is the real fix for the first. The outline froze because the gap rule pins placement to the *first* hole along the ray — break through and every subsequent placement still targets that hole. Mouse aiming replaces that guesswork with direct pointing.

The maths is the inverse of `project_point`, onto a horizontal plane:

Exact inverse, round-trips to the float. Now rewiring the preview to aim with the mouse:

The centre crosshair is now misleading — you aim with the mouse. Moving it there:

The cursor tracks the floor, respects reach, and won't build through a wall. Tests for the new maths:

The eye-height mutant survived — the same blind spot as before: every unproject test uses eye 0.5 over plane 0, so the height difference is always exactly 0.5.

Done and pushed (`37e91ba0`). Suite **2693 passed, 0 failed**.

**Your diagnosis was right.** The outline froze because the gap rule pins placement to the *first* gap along the centre ray — break through one wall and then the one behind it, and placement still points at the first hole. And your proposed fix is the better primitive: **pointing beats inferring**. A centre ray can only ever express "against the surface I'm facing"; it can't express *there*.

`unproject_to_plane` is the exact inverse of `project_point` onto a horizontal plane, so a screen position becomes the floor square it appears to be over. It round-trips to the float.

The walkaround now asks **two different questions**, because they genuinely are different:

- **Breaking** casts a ray through the mouse's *column*, still at eye height — with no pitch, mouse y can't tilt a ray, so only x steers it.
- **Building** unprojects the mouse onto the floor of your layer, so you point straight at the square you want.

A build cell also has to be empty, within reach, and **actually visible**. The floor of a square behind a wall still projects to a screen position, so without an occlusion check you could build through solid rock by pointing at where the floor would be. I checked it refuses.

The crosshair moved to the mouse — a fixed centre mark would point at a cell nothing acts on any more.

**To try it:** `B` for build mode, then move the mouse — the outline slides across the floor and follows it. Down the screen brings it closer, sideways moves it across. Right-click places where the outline is; left-click breaks whatever the crosshair is over.

## [2026-08-13] User

> how do I stack blocks one atop another?

### Claude

Let me verify what actually works right now rather than guess.

Verified rather than guessed — here's exactly what works.

**You stack by climbing.** Each placement lands on the floor plane level with your feet, so you gain height by standing on what you just built:

```
place a block in front of you     → (21,19,0)
walk onto it                       → you're now at layer 1
place again                        → (22,19,1)
walk onto that                     → layer 2
place again                        → (23,19,2)
```

The general rule, and it's worth internalising because everything follows from it: **you can place onto any surface that is level with your feet.** Standing on the terrace at layer 5, you can drop blocks onto anything whose top is at z=5. Standing on the ground, only onto the ground.

**What you cannot currently do is build a vertical column.** I confirmed it — standing at layer 0 and pointing at the block in front of you returns no build cell at any mouse position, because from layer 0 the only reachable surface is layer 0's floor, and that cell is occupied. So what you actually get from the sequence above is a *rising diagonal*, one block forward per block up — and those blocks are floating, since nothing supports them. A solid tower can't be built by hand.

That's a real gap, not a 2c limitation, and the fix is now cheap. **Mouse y already gives a genuine 3D ray** — the level pitch only means the camera's forward *axis* is horizontal; individual pixels still correspond to rays sloping up and down, which is exactly what `unproject_to_plane` exploits. So instead of intersecting one floor plane, the pick could march that ray through the voxel grid in 3D and return the first surface it meets, plus which face. That gives you the standard behaviour — point at the top of a block to stack on it, at a side face to build outward, and it also solves digging down and refilling a hole at the top of a wall, which the gap heuristic can't reach.

No renderer change needed; it's picking only. Want me to do that as the next unit? It'd replace the gap heuristic with something you can actually see and aim at.

- You must stand a couple of cells back for a ray to reach another layer.
- You can **never** see the top of a block on your *own* layer — its top sits half a cell above your eye. So the block directly in front of you at ground level still can't be stacked on; back up first, or climb.
- You can't dig straight down.

That's the concrete cost of deferring Phase 2c, and it's in the plan doc now instead of a vague "no looking up or down". If building a tower from where you stand turns out to matter, 2c is the answer and there's now a number to justify it.

## [2026-08-13] User

### Claude

Now the action, in the file that has the dispatch harness:

All six caught. Full suite:

Now the docs — including correcting the plan's own cost estimate for 2c:

The plan doc's opening paragraph got ragged from my status replacement — tidying that:

Now updating the status page, since 2c is no longer deferred:

**2c is done** (`c2da5605`). You can look up and down. Suite **2721 passed, 0 failed**; status page updated at the same link.

**The plan's estimate for this phase was wrong, and I've left the wrong text in the doc as a quote so it's checkable.** It said 2c was "the expensive step — real 3D ray marching, not the 2D-plus-height approximation of 2b". No 3D ray marching was needed in the renderer at all.

Two facts collapsed it, and I found them by grepping before designing anything:

1. **`half_h` appeared 16 times and every single one was the horizon reference** in the projection. Nothing horizontal depended on it.
2. **Pitch doesn't change azimuth.** A screen column still corresponds to the same ray, so the horizontal DDA never has to learn about it.

So looking up and down is a **y-shear**: slide the horizon, leave every other formula alone. `horizon = h/2 + h·tan(pitch)` — the vertical focal length works out to exactly the screen height in pixels. The rename was most of the work.

**What it buys, measured:** the steepest ray goes from 0.5 cells per cell (~26°) at level to ~2.2 (~65°) at −60° pitch. Digging down and building overhead both work now — that 26° ceiling was the thing the last three rounds of your feedback kept running into.

**One honest caveat, in the docs rather than glossed:** it's a shear, not a rotated camera. Vertical edges stay vertical instead of converging, exactly as Doom worked. For a world made of cubes I think the parallel edges read better than true perspective would, but it's an approximation and shouldn't be described as a 3D camera.

Picking followed for free, since `screen_ray` reads its slope from `sy - horizon`. The overlay functions take `horizon` as an optional argument defaulting to screen centre, so **all 162 existing Block World tests passed straight through the change** — that's the backward-compatibility evidence, not an assertion.

**To try it:** scroll the wheel (or `R`/`F`) to look around. Stand on the terrace and look down into the pit — the thing the preview kept as evidence of what deferring 2c cost. `set_look_pitch` is the action for authored games, absolute or relative for a held control.

Phase 3 is what's left in flight: the hotbar, and a committed world generator.

Root cause confirmed and it isn't the picking — **the eye sits at 0.5, inside the block layer**, so a neighbouring block's top at z=1.0 is *above* your eye. You're looking at its underside, and no amount of pitch fixes that. At 1.5 — a two-block-tall player, as every block game uses — it works at any pitch.

Nothing ships on Block World yet, so changing this default is free now and wouldn't be after Phase 5:

15 failures, all geometry tests that hand-compute against eye 0.5. Those are testing the projection, not the default — so I'll pin the eye height where the arithmetic depends on it, and add explicit coverage of the new default.

All 177 pass. Two consistency fixes the new default demands — the crosshair now sits a layer higher than the feet, so picking must follow it:

Now a test class that covers the shipped default — including the exact thing you couldn't do:

Fixed and pushed (`068d5ba3`). Suite **2725 passed, 0 failed**. You can stack blocks now.

**It wasn't picking, and 2c couldn't have fixed it.** A block beside you has its top face at z = 1, and the eye sat at 0.5 — *below* that surface. You were looking at its underside. The face points away from you at every pitch, so no amount of looking down helps. I measured it directly: sweeping the whole screen at pitch 0, −20 and −45 with a 0.5 eye, the cell on top of a neighbouring block is **never** offered; with a 1.5 eye it's offered at every pitch.

So the camera is now a **two-block-tall body** — `DEFAULT_EYE_HEIGHT = 1.5`, which is what every block game uses and, it turns out, exactly why. Two things had to move with it or the view and the actions would disagree:

- **The actions now address the layer the eye is in**, not the layer your feet are on. A level crosshair points at eye height; picking the feet layer would break a block the crosshair isn't on.
- The walkaround reads `eye_height` from the config instead of assuming 0.5 — otherwise it would have aimed a whole layer low.

**One thing worth flagging, because it has a deadline.** Changing a default like this is free *only* because no sample ships on this engine yet. After Phase 5 it would be a migration. If any other default is in doubt, it's worth settling before Phase 5 rather than after — that's now in the plan doc.

On the tests: the geometry tests deliberately pin `eye_height: 0.5`, because their closed-form assertions are 1:1 with layer numbers that way and 2a's recorded look was a mid-layer eye. `TestDefaultEyeHeight` covers the shipped value instead — including a **control proving the old value genuinely could not stack**, so the fix is pinned rather than just accommodated. All three mutants caught.

Try it: stand next to a block, tilt down slightly, and the outline should appear on its top face.

**The finding that reframes your list: this project ships three different engines.**

| What you run | Engine |
|---|---|
| Test Game in the IDE | `runtime/run_game.py` → pygame — complete, 3327 tests |
| .exe / Linux / macOS / Android | `export/Kivy/` — a **second** hand-written engine, "80% compatible" by its own docstring |
| HTML5 | `engine.js` + Pyodide — a **third** |

All four desktop/mobile exporters share one Kivy base. So **issues 4–8 aren't five bugs — they're five symptoms of "the thing you export isn't the thing you tested."** That's exactly why they present as four whole broken subsystems: tiles, keyboard, collision, physics. Each was independently reimplemented.

There are ~20 export test files and **not one plays a sample** — they all assert on generated strings and file layout. That's how six broken exports stayed green. It's the failure mode already in my memory as *"every export target needs an end-to-end play-a-sample test."*

**So my recommendation is to stop repairing the second engine and freeze the real one.** `runtime/run_game.py` is already a standalone entry point that the IDE launches as a subprocess — freezing *that* with PyInstaller makes the exported game the same program Test Game runs. It closes 4–8 in one change plus every export bug not yet found, and ends the drift permanently. The main risk is written down: plugin discovery globs `__file__`-relative directories and needs `_MEIPASS`-aware roots under a frozen app. Android would keep Kivy, since pygame doesn't target it.

Three smaller notes:

- **Issue 11 confirmed exactly** — `obj_goal` and `obj_key` both point at `spr_key` in raycast_4.
- **Issue 1 I deliberately did not guess at.** Three different samples all black means it fails before any per-sample difference. Step one is capturing the browser console under headless Chromium — the deliverable is the error, not a speculative fix.

That reasoning is weaker for a bundled sample handed to a French child — but I'd rather reverse it deliberately than quietly.

**Four decisions before I start:**

1. **Desktop engine** — freeze pygame (recommended) or repair Kivy?
2. **The short name** — "2.5 D" is short, accurate, and needs no translation in any of the 9 languages, which is a real advantage. Worth a moment on whether it reads as jargon to a 10-year-old; "3D" or "Labyrinthe 3D" are alternatives. Applies to all four raycast samples.

**Your priority 4 settles the export-engine question.** "Mobile uses Kivy, so we need that part as well" means **Kivy must be repaired regardless** — nothing else targets Android/iOS. So the question was never "Kivy or pygame". It's only: *which engine do the desktop executables ship?*

Given that, I've resolved it as **freeze the pygame runtime for desktop**:

- Priority 3 (the Debian and Mac labs) then arrives by *packaging* an engine with 3327 tests, rather than by bringing a second engine up to parity — and that second engine **can't be executed in CI at all**, so its correctness could only ever be asserted, never shown. That sits badly with "absolutely bug free".
- It decouples the school labs from mobile. Kivy's repair happens at priority 4 instead of blocking priority 3.

**What it does not do, stated plainly:** those four subsystems (tiles, keyboard, collision, physics) are *mobile* bugs too. After this, mobile exports stay exactly as broken as today. That's a consequence of mobile being priority 4, not a side benefit.

**Issue 2 turned out to be the opposite of what I estimated, in both directions.**

- **There is no mechanism to localise authored strings at all.** The runtime translates its own UI — window caption, high-score table — but `show_message` text comes straight out of `project.json` as a literal. So this *cannot* be fixed by typing French; the engine has nowhere to put it. It's ~90% engine work.

**I hit a real trap doing it, and it's worth knowing about.** The first render showed six lines of `0`:

`ActionExecutor._parse_value` routes any string containing `* + - / %` to the **expression evaluator**, so `draw_text` reading `"W A S D  -  Move"` gets *evaluated*, not displayed. The escape hatch is a leading double quote inside the JSON, which `_parse_value` returns verbatim.

What makes this worth recording rather than just fixing: **existing sample text had only ever avoided it by accident.** `Lives:` and `M = map` use `:` and `=`, which aren't operators. block_world_1's help was the first sample text containing a dash, and the first to break. So it was waiting for whoever next wrote a sentence on screen.

**D2a done** (`e50ed12d`). Suite **3432 passed, 0 failed**; smoke 20/20.

**I have to correct my own plan on this one.** It claimed there was no mechanism for localising authored strings. There is, and it predates this work:

- `events/action_editor.py` has offered a translation dialog for **any** string parameter since it was written, storing `<param>_translations` dicts.
- But the runtime read **only `message_translations`, only for `show_message`**.

So an author could enter translations for a `draw_text` and the engine would **silently ignore them** — the IDE promised what the engine didn't deliver. That's now closed for `message`, `text` and `caption`. `draw_score`'s caption had to be localised as a *parameter*, because it's concatenated with the score before reaching the draw queue.

The fallbacks mattered most, since 20 samples carry no translations: no dict, unknown language, empty string, non-dict value, or no runner all leave the English exactly as before.

**And I found the real blocker for issue 2, which is now confirmed rather than suspected.** Neither export engine reads these dicts — `engine.js`'s `show_message` uses `params.message` only, and `export/Kivy/` has no mention of translations at all. Notably, `tests/test_raycast_2_sample.py` *encodes that as a reason not to translate samples*: **"keeping the sample free of a translation dict means it behaves identically on every export target."** On the facts, that argument was right.

So typing French now would fix the desktop and leave a French student's HTML5 export — your priority 2 — still in English. The answer is **export-time resolution**: bake the chosen language's string into the exported project. That needs no translation support in either engine, can't diverge from the desktop, and keeps both currently-broken engines out of it.

## [2026-08-17] User

> Approach this as the design lead at a small studio known for their versatility, giving every client a visual identity pitched at the treatment the task actually calls for. Make deliberate choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this subject, and avoid templated designs.
> 
> ## Read the request first
> 
> Calibrate treatment, not whether to design. A doc deserves the same craft as a landing page — what changes is the treatment that craft is delivered in. Format is part of this read — decided, not defaulted: a Markdown publish keeps its filename as its title and takes almost none of the craft below, so it fits only when the user asked for Markdown or the content is bound for a Markdown-native destination; never pick it to save time.
> 
> Many requests call for a more utilitarian treatment: a plan, a memo, a demo. Make it polished: include real typographic hierarchy, considered spacing, and a proper palette, but avoid over-designing. Most pages do not need a flashy, gigantic hero. Keep flourishes tasteful and limited.
> 
> Some requests call for an editorial treatment: a landing page, a game, an app or tool they'll keep or share.
> 
> When unsure: a well-composed page is never the wrong answer; an over-designed visual identity sometimes is.
> 
> Fundamentals below apply to everything. The editorial process after that runs only when the read above says so.
> 
> ## Fundamentals for every artifact
> 
> **Honor what's already there** Look for an existing design system first — CLAUDE.md, a tokens or theme file, existing component styles. When one exists, apply it; everything below fills gaps and never overrides. Precedence is always: the user's own words, then the project's existing system, then your choices.
> 
> **Ground it in the subject.** If the subject isn't already clear, pin it: one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job. The subject's own world — its materials, instruments, vernacular — is where distinctive choices come from. Build with real content throughout, never lorem.
> 
> **Pair typefaces** Typography carries the page even when the page isn't about typography. The Artifact CSP blocks font CDNs, so don't link a webfont URL and risk a silent fallback. Instead inline the face as a @font-face data URI. Keep running text near 65 characters wide; set a type scale and stay on it; give headings `text-wrap: balance`, body text room to breathe, and uppercase labels a touch of letter-spacing.
> 
> **Choose neutrals, don't default to them.** A pure mid-grey reads as unconsidered; a grey with a slight hue bias toward the page's accent reads as chosen. Pure white and near-black are fine grounds when they suit the subject — the point is that the neutral was picked, not inherited.
> 
> **Design both themes.** The page renders in the viewer's theme, and the viewer has three states, not two: an explicit choice stamps `data-theme="dark"` / `data-theme="light"` on the root element, and the default "system" setting stamps *nothing* — most viewers see the un-stamped document, where only `prefers-color-scheme` separates light from dark. Structure the CSS token-level for all three: the bare `:root` block defines the complete light palette (for a deliberately dark-first design, swap light and dark consistently through this whole pattern); `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` redefines only the tokens, guarded as `:root:not([data-theme="light"])` so an explicit light choice beats a dark OS; `:root[data-theme="dark"]` redefines them again so the toggle also wins in the other direction. Style components through the tokens, never directly inside a media or `[data-theme]` block — a color whose only definition sits behind `[data-theme]` never applies in the un-stamped state, and the page renders one theme's text on the other theme's ground. Two more rules keep each theme resolving as a set: the artifact composites over a ground the viewer paints in *its* theme, so `body` must set an explicit `background` from a token — a transparent body silently borrows the host's ground; and every element that sets a color takes it from the same token set as the surface behind it, never a literal that only works in one theme. Before publishing, scan the stylesheet for any color declared only inside a media or `[data-theme]` block — that is the classic unreadable-artifact bug. Give the second theme the same care as the first — don't naively invert; keep contrast legible and the accent working on both grounds. A design that deliberately commits to one visual world (a neon arcade screen, a letterpress invitation) may stay single-theme — then skip the media query and stamps entirely but still paint the background and every color explicitly, so the page holds on either host ground; make it a choice, not an omission.
> 
> **Let layout do the spacing.** Lay out sibling groups with flex or grid and `gap`, not per-element margins that silently collapse or double. Wide content — tables, code, diagrams — gets `overflow-x: auto` on its own container so the page body never scrolls sideways. Reach for `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` wherever digits line up in columns.
> 
> **Avoid AI-generated design** AI-generated design currently clusters around a few looks: warm cream (#F4F1EA) with a serif display and terracotta accent; near-black with a lone acid-green or vermilion pop; broadsheet hairline rules with dense columns; a purple-to-blue gradient hero on white; Inter or Space Grotesk as the "safe" face; emoji as section markers; everything centered; `rounded-lg` everywhere; accent bar/rail on rounded cards. Where the user pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly — their words always win, including when they ask for one of these looks. Where nothing is specified, don't spend that freedom on one of these defaults.
> 
> **Build cleanly** Be cognizant of overlapping elements, cascade collisions, silent font fallbacks; visual bugs hide in the gap between source and output. Close every non-void element, double-quote attributes, give keyboard focus a visible state, respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. For generative or decorative graphics, reach for Canvas or WebGL rather than hand-authoring long SVG path data.
> 
> **CSS rules** When writing the CSS, watch your selector specificities. It is easy to generate classes that cancel each other out — a type-based selector like `.section` fighting an element-based one like `.cta` over padding and margins between sections. Structure the cascade so it doesn't silently undo your spacing.
> 
> **Writing the copy** Words are design material, not decoration. Write from the user's side of the screen — name things by what people recognize, not how the system is built (a person manages *notifications*, not *webhook config*). Active voice; a control says exactly what happens ("Publish", then a toast that says "Published"). Errors explain what went wrong and how to fix it — no apologies, no vagueness. Specific beats clever.
> 
> **Name the page like a product, not a caption.** The `<title>` is the artifact's name in the gallery and the browser tab, and it sets the reader's first impression of care. Give the page a real name: a short noun phrase, typically two to four words, specific to the subject — or, for a page that exists to answer one question, that question itself, which is then the page's name. Stop at the name — a title that carries its own explainer after a dash or colon reads as generated filler. The name must also identify the page among many: in the gallery it sits beside dozens of other artifacts, and a generic category label that could sit on any of them fails as a name just as surely as an appended explainer. When a candidate title pairs the name with a generic word — a greeting, a category, a page-type label — the name is the half to keep; a trim that drops the identity and keeps the generic word produces exactly the title that could sit on any page. And the rule removes explainers, it does not impose brevity: a multi-word title that already reads as one specific name is finished, and shortening it further only makes it generic. The one-sentence publish `description` is where the explanation belongs; the gallery shows it right under the title.
> 
> **Structure is information** Structural devices, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, should encode something true about the content, not decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that's only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence - like a real process or a typed timeline where order carries information the reader needs. Question if choices like numbered markers actually make sense before incorporating them.
> 
> **When it's a UI, not a document** A dashboard or tool is scanned and operated, not read top-to-bottom, so the craft shifts from typography to information design. Surface the summary before the detail; encode state in form as well as number — a pill, a chip, a severity stripe — so what needs attention reads at a glance. Semantic color (good / warning / critical) is separate from the accent hue and doesn't count as your accent. Give sparklines and charts the same care as type: an area fill, a faint grid, an emphasized endpoint. What's interactive should look interactive.
> 
> 
> 
> ## Process
> 
> Before writing code, sketch a short design plan — a compact token system with color, type, and layout:
> - **Color**: describe the palette as 4–6 named hex values.
> - **Type**: typefaces for 2+ roles — a characterful display face used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed.
> - **Layout**: a layout concept in one or two sentences.
> 
> Then build, following the plan and deriving every color and type decision from it.
> 
> ## When the request is editorial
> 
> The stance shifts: the client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view. Make opinionated calls, and take one real aesthetic risk where it serves the work.
> 
> Review the design plan against the subject before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page, revise that part, and note what you changed and why. Only after you've confirmed the plan's uniqueness do you write the code, following the revised plan exactly.
> 
> **Principles** 
> 
> - The hero is a thesis: open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world — headline, image, live demo, interactive moment. 
> - Typography carries the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, not the same families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content. 
> - Leverage motion deliberately. Think about where and if animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, a scroll-triggered reveal, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. An orchestrated moment usually lands harder than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation contributes to the feeling that the design is AI-generated. 
> - Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well.
> - Spend your boldness in one place; keep everything around it quiet. If the accent fights the ground, shift it toward analogous or drop saturation rather than replacing it.

## What changed

**Desktop exports now run the real engine.** The `.exe`/Linux/`.app` exporters bundled the Kivy code generator, a second engine self-described as "80% GameMaker 7.0 compatible" — so an export was never the game you tested. Making that measurable needed two opt-in engine hooks (`PYGM_MAX_FRAMES`, `PYGM_SCREENSHOT`), because a compiled binary can't be measured the way `smoke_run_samples.py` measures the samples, and "still running after 20 seconds" can't tell a working game from one stuck on a black screen. ## Subsystem 1 — tiles (`0d8196de`)

The exporter never read `room['tiles']`. plateforme_2 has 127 and plateforme_3 has 125, so both exported as a bare background with objects floating over it. The gap survived review because the only prior mention of "tiles" in the file was about repeating a background *image* — same word, unrelated feature.

The interesting part was two independent y-flips: a tile's room position flips against `room_height`, while its crop offset within the source sheet flips against the *texture* height, because GameMaker measures from the image top and Kivy's `get_region` from the bottom. I mutation-tested each one — getting either wrong puts the art somewhere plausible rather than failing.

## Subsystem 2 — keyboard (`0359d3f1`)

Issue 5 ("arrow keys move until a wall, then stuck") had three separate causes, and the decisive one wasn't the one I set out to fix:

**`start_moving_direction` was iterating a string as a list.** Samples store the direction as a plain name, so `"right"` was read as five characters, each an unrecognised direction defaulting to 0. Every arrow key set direction 0 — so the player only ever moved right, hit a wall, and looked stuck. The character counts match the symptom exactly: right=5, down=4, left=4, up=2.

Beyond that: held keyboard events were bound to Kivy's key-down instead of firing every frame as GameMaker does, so movement stuttered at the OS repeat rate; and held `keyboard` and one-shot `keyboard_press` both generated a method named `on_keyboard`, so an object with one of each had the second silently shadow the first.

**Issue 6 is also explained.** `anykey` compiled to `if key == 0:`, which no keycode matches. maze_4's start screen advances on `anykey → next_room` — hence "shows the background, won't start on space." Dead handler, now dispatched.

Still open: **collision** and **physics/sub-images**. Neither has been investigated yet, and given how these two went, I'd expect the reported symptoms to be surface descriptions again rather than the actual defects.

## Where things stand

**All twelve eyeball issues and both deliverables are closed.** Desktop exports now freeze the real pygame engine, verified 5/5 by building, launching and pixel-comparing against the IDE; the cross-platform checklist is written, tested and published.

I found collision's root cause — `_process_movement` blocks only when the *moving* instance is solid, where desktop requires just one of the pair, so a non-solid player walks through solid walls (issue 4, half of issue 7). I stopped short of fixing it.

The one-line change would make things worse. Kivy reverts *both* axes on collision; desktop resolves per axis and slides to contact. Gravity pushes into the floor every frame, so enabling the block naively would cancel horizontal movement too — trading "falls through the floor" for "can't walk." Landing that at 86% usage, without budget to test a platformer actually walking, would have been the wrong call. I run **on this machine**. My Bash/Read/Edit tools act on this filesystem, in `c:\Users\gthul\pygm`. For anything to happen, three things must all be true: the computer is on and awake, VSCode is open, and this session is alive. Sleep or hibernate counts as off. A scheduled job would die with the session, so programming one and shutting down would silently do nothing — which is the worst outcome, because you'd come back expecting progress.

## Your realistic options

**Just resume manually when you're back.** This is what I'd recommend, and the whole session has been run so that it costs you nothing: every unit is committed and pushed, the tree is clean, and `docs/EYEBALL_FIXES_2026-08-16.md` A2.3 holds the collision diagnosis with both file locations and the required shape of the fix. **Leave it awake and schedule a one-shot.** Possible — say the word and I'll set a job for just after your limit resets. But it needs the machine awake (check your power settings, not just the screen), VSCode open, and this session untouched. That's a lot of preconditions for a 20-minute wait.

**Claude Code on the web** (claude.ai/code) runs in Anthropic's cloud rather than on your hardware — that's the genuine "computer off" answer, and your repo is fully pushed to GitHub so it has everything it needs. You'd start that yourself from a browser or phone; I can't launch it from here.

## One caveat about the next task specifically

The next unit is the Kivy collision fix, and it's the one I'd least want running unattended. I stopped precisely because the obvious fix would trade "falls through the floor" for "can't walk," and verifying it needs judgement about whether a platformer actually plays right — the kind of thing where you'd want to interject. ## What landed

The diagnosis I committed before shutting down held up, and building to it was the right call. The fix has three parts, and the middle one is why the one-liner would have been wrong:

**The blocking rule** now matches the desktop engine, and it needed *two* conditions rather than the one my diagnosis named: a collision event must exist between the pair **and** at least one must be solid. So two non-solid objects still overlap freely — that's how a maze monster runs through the player instead of standing on its head — and a solid wall with no collision event doesn't block, which is GameMaker 7.0 behaviour.

**Per-axis resolution with a vertical slide-to-contact.** This is what the deferral was about. Gravity presses the player into the floor every frame, so reverting both axes would have cancelled horizontal movement too — "can't walk" instead of "falls through the floor." I wrote the walking-along-the-ground test first, then built a genuine both-axes-revert mutant: it's caught by exactly the three per-axis tests. The naive fix would not have shipped.

**One thing the diagnosis missed:** blocked moves have to fire the pair's collision handlers. Blocking prevents the overlap that the post-move detection pass looks for, so plateforme_2's authored `move_to_contact` + `set_hspeed(0)` would never have run, leaving the player pressed against the wall with its speed still set.

Worth noting: the desktop engine's own comment records hitting the vertical-gap problem — a fast faller left hanging with its animation cycling. That's the other half of issue 8's "Pingus placed too high," so this fix reaches into A2.4 territory as well.

## A2.4, this session's work

Two findings, and the first was bigger than the symptom suggested.

**The vertical sign convention was applied inconsistently.** Instances in a generated Kivy game live in Kivy coordinates (y *up*) — the exporter establishes that when it places them, turning plateforme_2's authored GM y=576 in a 640-tall room into y=32. Most of the exporter knew this; `move_grid`'s map is even commented "Kivy Y is inverted". The physics layer didn't, having been copied from the desktop runtime where y grows downward. Three consequences:

- `gravity_direction` 270 ("down") pulled everything **up** — "Pingus rises"
- every direction-driven vertical move was inverted, so an up arrow moved down
- `if_collision`'s y offset probed the ceiling instead of the floor, so a platformer's jump could never find ground

**`set_sprite` silently discarded expression sub-images.** It ran `int(params['subimage'])` and dropped the assignment in the except branch, so plateforme_3's `subimage=random(image_number)` left all 52 bonuses on frame 0. The desktop runtime hit and documented this same class of bug, so I mirrored its fix — the base object now provides `image_number`, `random`, `irandom`, `choose`.

Suite 3622 → **3655 passed, 0 failed**. Thirteen mutants tried, thirteen caught.

## A correction to my own earlier work

My subsystem-2 commit message claimed Kivy keeps GameMaker y-down coordinates and that direction 90 is up "as the desktop does." The direction *values* were right; the coordinate space claim was wrong, so 90 moved the player **down**. My test asserted the stored value and passed while the motion was inverted — the third time this session that a value-level assertion hid a behavioural bug. Every test in this unit asserts *screen direction* instead. The misleading docstring is fixed and the correction is recorded in the commit.

## What's genuinely still open

**Nobody has played an exported mobile build.** Every fix was verified by executing the generated code under stub Kivy modules and compiling every sample's output — real evidence, but not the same as running it on a phone, and Kivy can't run in CI. I rewrote the checklist's mobile section accordingly: it went from "expect this broken" to eight rows each naming what specifically used to be wrong, so you can tell a regression from a vague feeling. Same for desktop — the exports are proven to render identical frames, not to feel right under a held key.

### Claude

There's precedent here — `CLAUDE.md` records that `6753ac1a` was already merged and deleted in commit `6b14f7f`. Let me check before acting:

`2026-07-22-6753ac1a.md` is a **regenerated duplicate** — the `<!-- curated -->` marker protects the file it's *in*, not the source it was merged *from*. Let me verify containment before deleting anything:

The merged section runs 8036→10518 (2482 lines) vs the standalone's 2581. Let me check whether the standalone has content the merge lacks:

Tails differ, so let me diff properly rather than guess:

Now it's clear: the standalone is identical for 2479 lines, then has **93 extra lines** — the 2026-07-29 exchange where the consolidation itself was decided, which by definition couldn't be in the merged content. Let me check the extractor before fixing:

The extractor recreates any file lacking the `<!-- curated -->` marker — deleting it just brings it back. Let me consolidate the 93-line delta and leave a tombstone that stops the regeneration at source:

Let me verify the extractor's head window actually covers the marker's position:

Both protected. Committing:

Working tree is clean for the first time this session. Pushed as `bb97faf0`.

The two files turned out to be different problems, so I handled them differently.

**`2026-07-22-6753ac1a.md` was a ghost.** `CLAUDE.md` records it merged into `2026-07-03-884fd424.md` and deleted back in `6b14f7f` — yet there it was again. The cause is a neat trap: `scripts/export_session_notes.py` skips a file only if it *already carries* the `<!-- curated -->` marker, and a deleted file has no marker to find, so the extractor just rewrote the whole transcript. Deleting it again would have repeated the loop indefinitely.

Diffing the regenerated copy against the merged section showed the first 2479 lines identical and **93 extra at the end** — the 2026-07-29 exchange in which that first merge was decided, which by definition couldn't exist inside the merge itself. So the earlier consolidation was very slightly incomplete. Those lines are now appended to the curated file, and the standalone became a 16-line tombstone carrying the marker: it explains where the transcript went *and* stops the regeneration at source. I verified that with the extractor's exact guard — first 500 characters, whole-line match — rather than assuming the marker would be seen.

**`2026-08-13-d75927de.md` was genuinely new** — this session's own transcript, 54 exchanges covering the desktop-export rework onto the pygame engine, the platform checklist, and all four Kivy subsystem repairs. Committed as-is, matching every other file in that directory.

Worth knowing for next time: that directory will show this session's file as modified again as the conversation continues, since the extractor keeps appending to it. That's normal, not a regression — and the tombstone means only *one* file will show up, not two.

### Claude

That's the `python3` landmine — on Windows it resolves to the Microsoft Store stub. `CLAUDE.md` documents this; my checklist only spelled out the Windows form for pytest and left the other commands bare. My mistake. The right command:

**20/20 samples OK**, no crashes. Now let me fix the checklist so this doesn't bite again:

Now a test to pin it, since this class of error is easy to reintroduce:

Let me confirm the test actually catches the bug it's meant to, then update the published artifact:

The published artifact has the same bug. Let me fix it there too:

My checklist was wrong, and you found it on the very first command. Fixed and pushed (`9136b40f`).

## The command you wanted

```
py -3.12 tools/smoke_run_samples.py
```

I ran it: **20/20 samples ran with no fatal loop crash** — every maze, platformer, match3, views, all four 2.5 D levels, both block worlds, multiplayer and treasure, 180 frames each. That's the first automated row on the checklist ticked for Windows.

## What went wrong

I split Windows and Linux forms for the `pytest` line and then left the other three commands as bare `python3`. `CLAUDE.md` documents this landmine explicitly and I still shipped it.

Worth knowing: the stub message you got is the *good* outcome. If the App Execution Alias is turned off, `python3` resolves to an unsupported 3.14 instead — the command runs, then fails in confusing ways. Failing loudly was lucky.

## Fixed

The commands block is now two complete sets, Windows and Linux/macOS, with the failure named so a reader who hits it doesn't assume their own install is broken. The served-directory command in the HTML5 section got the same treatment.

Two tests pin it: every tool the checklist names must appear in **both** a `py -3.12` and a `python3` form, and the Microsoft Store stub must be named. I mutation-checked by reverting one command to bare `python3` — caught.

The published artifact is updated at the same URL: **https://claude.ai/code/artifact/4f7e1220-f9e0-4cb6-b073-42581ddd80ba**

Suite 3726 passed, 0 failed.

One note on that number: it jumped from 3658 to 3726 without me adding 68 tests. Other work has been landing in this repo alongside mine — I can see new signing code in the macOS exporter and a `version` field in its `Info.plist` that I didn't write. Nothing is failing, so it's not a problem, but if that isn't you working in another session it's worth knowing about.

Next automated row when you're ready: `py -3.12 tools/verify_desktop_export.py --all --compare` — about a minute per sample.

A verification tool that cries wolf trains you to ignore it, so let me fix both. First the dialog:

Their fix is functionally identical to mine — same guard, same early return, and it landed first with its own tests. So I'll keep theirs and keep only my unique part, the seed hook:
